# WP-F decision drafts (Sync: planner and engine)

Stage 2, level L4, branch `wp/f`. One entry per non-trivial decision, in the `DECISIONS.md` shape (context · options · decision · consequences); the orchestrator numbers them at merge. Measurements were taken on this host (Apple silicon, macOS 27) in this session, with other worktrees building beside it (load average about 4).

**Proposed REENTRANCY rows** (the code cites them as "f-n (proposed)"; every other `// REENTRANCY:` in `ZTronAssetSync` cites an RE-S row of ARCHITECTURE §10.2):

| Row | Where | What is re-validated after the await |
|---|---|---|
| f-1 | `runStart`: `store.setRetentionScope` (after `open()`, and again in a loop just before the engine is marked running), `store.makeStagingSink` | The lifecycle (shutdown may have begun). `setRetentionScope` while start runs only replaces the engine's copy, so start pushes again while the sequence it pushed is not the newest; the engine is marked running in the turn of the last comparison, after which every scope pushes itself (fix round 1, F-18). A failed `makeStagingSink` degrades the engine (no start task is left to settle later callers). |
| f-2 | `drive`/`resumePoint`: sidecar reads for the next attempt's resume point | The lifecycle and the job epoch; the resume point is kept only for the job (and attempt) that read it. Since close-out cycle 10 (F-30), in `paused` also the lane's block under the conditions then current: a policy pause parks the job only while its lane is still refused (with the block it gives now), and re-queues it at the front otherwise. |
| f-3 | `staging.discard(_:)` of copies and partials nobody wants | Nothing: the removal is idempotent and completes whatever happens meanwhile; the callers' following steps re-read the job by epoch. |
| f-4 | `refreshFreeSpace`, `spaceFailed`: `store.freeSpace()`; since close-out cycle 6 (F-26) also `settleSpaceWaitsOnFreshRead` (the read that decides jobs parked `awaitingSpace` with no wake left) and `spaceMayHaveBeenReclaimed` (after a purge) | The lifecycle, and in `spaceFailed` the job epoch; the headroom ledger's base is replaced whole by the fresh read (S2-09: committed bytes stay until the next read). In `settleSpaceWaitsOnFreshRead` every job's state (only those still `awaitingSpace` are decided) and the wakes armed meanwhile (a store deadline, the alarm's store work) are re-read after it; in `spaceMayHaveBeenReclaimed` the jobs still parked `awaitingSpace` are re-queued and gated on it. Since close-out cycle 8 (F-28) the read after the alarm's reaped bytes is cited here too, and these three read through `readFreeSpace` and then re-check every job parked for space in one sweep (`sweepSpaceParked(.reclaimed)` or `(.settle)`), which re-reads each job's state and the wakes. |
| f-5 | `performAwaitAsset`: `store.planningView()` before the join | The lifecycle; the record (quarantined, failed, present) decides T2 before the join, which is synchronous. | f-9 | `catchUpWithStore`: `store.planningView()` after the store superseded content the engine's copy still names (fix round 2, F-20) | The lifecycle; the copy is replaced only by a store view whose manifest differs from it (then T21/T22 follow, `followApplied`), and the held jobs are released in the same turn (`manifestCaughtUp`). Since close-out cycle 1 (L4-R7) the same turn also announces the ids the view no longer lists (`assetRemoved(.pruned)`), takes the view's generation, publishes `snapshotChanged`, and re-checks every remembered binding against the view (L4-R5). Since close-out cycle 3 (F-23) the view is followed only when no `apply` was in flight during the read and the copy did not change since it began (`appliesInFlight`, `copySerial`); otherwise the read is owed again (`catchUpAfterFailedApply`) once no `apply` is in flight. |
| f-10 | `failed` (`permanentMissing`): `store.location(for:extensions:recordingAccess:)` per id of the job, before `store.recordFailure` (close-out cycle 1, L4-R5); since close-out cycle 3 the same reads (`sparePresent`) before `store.recordIntegrityFailure` in `integrityFailed` (transport and publication integrity failures, F-23) | The lifecycle and the job epoch: the ids the store serves at their current content (a location that is not stale) while the engine's copy maps them to the failed content are answered from the store (only the interests the job still holds then) and left out of the failure; the failure is recorded for the job's other ids whether or not the job still exists; the present ids' bindings are recorded only when no binding was forgotten during the reads. A binding the store makes between the read and the store write is not seen by the engine; since close-out cycle 11 the store's guard leaves such a record unchanged in `recordFailure` (L4-R16, F-31), while `recordIntegrityFailure` keeps the window (F-21). |
| f-11 | `drive` → `answeredFromStore`: `store.location(for:extensions:recordingAccess:)` per id of the job, before `remote.transfer` (close-out cycle 3, F-23) | The lifecycle, the job epoch and the attempt (still without a handle): the ids the store serves at their current content while the engine's copy maps them to this content are answered from the store (only the interests the job holds then); an attempt left with no interest ends before it asks for bytes (marked terminal, so no drain is recorded, and retired); any other outcome (a job no longer current, an absent id) goes on to `remote.transfer`, whose existing RE-S12 checks apply. Bindings are recorded only when no binding was forgotten during the reads. |
| f-12 | `performReportUnreadable` → `blobHolders`: `store.planningView()` and the off-actor scan of its records for the reported id's blob, before `store.reportUnreadable` (close-out cycle 3, F-23) | Nothing: the holders are a snapshot; after the quarantine each one is re-read from the records `scheduleHeals` reads (its RE-S30 read) and announced and healed only if quarantined or failed then; the ledger is dropped whatever the snapshot found. |
| f-13 | `applyAndFollow` → `takeApplyTurn`: waiting for the engine's apply turn (close-out cycle 5, F-25) | The lifecycle and the turn: resumed once by `passApplyTurn` (after the previous `apply` was followed) or by shutdown (`releaseApplyQueue`); the loop re-reads the turn (an `apply` that never waited may have taken it first; the waiter keeps its ticket) and an `apply` whose turn came after the engine stopped running sends nothing to the store (`.failure(.closed)`). |
| f-14 | `joinGroup`: the `Task.yield()` between batches of a prefetch group's members (close-out cycle 8, F-28) | The lifecycle, the source and the group still registered (a release drops it); the engine's manifest copy: a changed copy sends the members left through a fresh `planningView()` (this function again), whose view is followed only when the read cannot predate the copy. Each member's binding is checked as before (L4-R5). |

---

## F-1 — One state value, owned tasks keyed by spawn id, token-keyed joins
- **Context.** ARCHITECTURE §7 and ADR-039/042: every awaiting caller must be resumed exactly once (by its result, its own cancellation or shutdown), and shutdown must join everything it owns.
- **Options.** (a) Per-feature actors (a run actor, a dispatcher actor); (b) one actor whose mutable state is one value (`EngineState`), with owned tasks and continuations in keyed tables.
- **Decision.** (b). `SyncEngine` keeps `var state: EngineState`; every owned task is spawned through `spawn(_:_:)` under a unique key and removes itself through `taskEnded` (shutdown cancels and joins them all); every suspended caller (waiters, unknown-id waiters, start/sync/entry joiners, groups, condition waits, run completions) sits in a table keyed by an `InterestToken` and is resumed only by `removeValue(forKey:)?.resume(...)`, so a second resume is impossible by construction. Cancellation handlers hop to the actor and withdraw only their own token. Overlapping access to `self.state` (Swift exclusivity) is avoided by computing into locals and writing back once, never by reading `state` inside a closure that mutates it.
- **Consequences.** No `@unchecked Sendable`, no locks (`UNSAFE escapes found=0`). `invariantViolations()` checks the cross-table invariants (waiter ⇒ interest, job ⇒ interest, downloading ⇔ active attempt, group member ⇒ interest, running ≤ maximum, lane-queue and subscriber-order bounds, no illegal transition, no stranded pending run); the 10,000-operation model test (`R-SYN-2`) checks them after every operation and `R-SYN-12: the invariant check reports every broken engine invariant it guards` proves the oracle is not vacuous.

## F-2 — The planner mirrors `apply`, and the space verdict is pure
- **Decision.** `PlanBuilder.plan` is a pure function of (manifest, planning view, scope, policy, free space): relocations are metadata-changed ids that have records; prunes are removed ids with records; a reuse needs the content among live blobs and a record that accepts adoption (never `absent(.evicted)`); held back are `failedUntilEntryChanges` and integrity quarantines; ineligible are out-of-scope, on-demand (unless the scope is explicit) and unverified entries the policy refuses. Fetch order is `(policy rank, manifest index)`. Space: zero bytes always fits; `room = min(volumeOpportunistic − reserve + pendingReclaim, max(0, budget − (used − pruneBytes)))`; a shortfall is `needsEviction(shortfall)` when eviction is permitted, else the largest fitting prefix (`partial`), else `insufficient(required: need + reserve, available: volumeOpportunistic)`. Sums saturate. A user-lane plan (`ensureAvailable`) plans against `volumeImportant` (sync-2 S2-23).
- **Consequences.** Planning runs off the actor (`@concurrent nonisolated static func planOffActor`, C2). P-4: 10,000 entries in 11.3 ms (budget 20), ratio 2.02 at 20,000 (max 2.5). When eviction cannot free the shortfall the engine replans with eviction not permitted, so an over-budget catalog still syncs its prefix (`R-SYN-11: a catalog larger than the budget syncs its largest fitting prefix essential-first and stays due`, which also shows the stamp is not written).

## F-3 — Run settling and pending runs (with a race fixed in this level)
- **Decision.** A run settles `finished` when every planned fetch ended, or `blocked(reason)` when every outstanding job is blocked or offline (`awaitingSpace` never settles a run). A blocked run drops its interests and leaves one pending run whose gate is the reason: conditions (debounced by `pendingRunDebounce`), breaker (cooldown deadline or close) or throttle (its end). `fulfilPendingRun` starts it only while no run exists.
- **Finding (test first).** The alarm that opens a gate can run before the blocked run's own task has finished it (both wait for the actor); `fulfilPendingRun` then found the run in place and returned, the deadline was consumed, and the pending run was stranded until the next explicit sync. Failing test `R-SYN-6: a pending run whose gate opens while the blocked run is still finishing starts once it has finished` (500 iterations with no debounce; failed 5 of 6 runs before the fix), committed before the fix together with the `strandedPendingRun` invariant. Fix: `reconsiderPendingRun()` after every run finishes and after every breaker record: an open gate starts the run, an unarmed one is re-armed (debounce, breaker deadline, throttle deadline, watcher).
- **Consequences.** The model test now also catches a stranded pending run. A breaker whose probe ended without a verdict (`halfOpen(probe: nil)`) lets the pending run probe. *Corrected in close-out cycle 20 (F-40):* such an exit also re-admits, in its turn, every job the breaker parked (the first dispatched probes), so a run whose claims they are never settles blocked by it; and a pending run starts only through `reconsiderPendingRun`, once its gate reads open at `now`.

## F-4 — The breaker half-opens lazily; manifest fetches are gated too
- **Decision.** `CircuitBreaker` is a pure value per origin: the 5xx cooldown is exact (`30 s · 2^(trips−1)` to 600 s), the auth cooldown is jittered to `[0.5c, c]` with keyed randomness (ADR-024); the open → half-open transition happens in `admit` (the first transfer admitted after the cooldown is the probe, with a watchdog), and the alarm's breaker deadline only unblocks jobs and fulfils the pending run. A manifest fetch goes through the same breaker and the origin throttle; a fetch that trips the breaker (a key-rejected 403) ends the run `blocked(.circuitOpen)` with a pending run, and a run started while the breaker is open asks for nothing (`R-SYN-5: a run started while the breaker is open ends blocked without asking for the manifest`).
- **Consequences.** `circuitChanged(.halfOpen)` is published when the probe is admitted, not at the cooldown's end. A transfer probe's admission was applied without being published (failing test first: `R-SYN-5: a transfer that probes after the cooldown publishes the half-open breaker before it closes`); it now goes through `breakerChanged` like a manifest probe's. Breaker events share one coalescing slot per origin (R-SYN-12), so a subscriber that pulls late may see `closed` without the `halfOpen` before it.

## F-5 — Store deadlines after a publication (F-12 of the level-3 notes)
- **Context.** `store.nextDeadline()` scans the blob table, so calling it after every publication made a bulk sync O(n²) (the 10,000-asset stalled-subscriber test took minutes).
- **Decision.** `refreshStoreDeadlineAfterPublish` skips the read when the alarm already wakes for the store within two snapshot-install intervals; `runDueWork()`'s report re-arms from the newest deadline then. Every other path (apply, purge, maintenance, launch) reads it.
- **Consequences.** A publication only ever adds work due within the store's coalescing windows, so nothing is missed; the 10,000-asset test runs in seconds.

## F-6 — Unknown identifiers: one point fetch per refetch interval, never a wrong notFound
- **Decision.** An identifier absent from the applied manifest joins (or starts) one manifest fetch; the fetched manifest's entry is admitted (`store.admit`, no ETag stored, sync-2 S2-28) and fetched without a run; an identifier the fresh manifest does not list is cached as unknown for `unknownIDRefetchInterval` (bounded LRU of `unknownIDCacheCapacity`). An unusable manifest answers `serverUnavailable`, a throttle `serverUnavailable(retryAfter:)`, an open breaker its cause.
- **Consequences.** `R-CAR-2: misspelt identifiers trigger at most one manifest fetch per refetch interval`; `R-CAR-2: an identifier a fresh manifest lists is admitted and fetched without a run`. B-30 holds the other way: the engine never skips `apply` on digest equality, so the admitted digest (which no fetched manifest can match) only ever causes an apply that would have happened anyway.

## F-7 — Launch reconciliation and staging claims (C-9)
- **Decision.** `TransferReconciler.plan` (pure, sorted by name): attach the newest running task of a wanted transfer, cancel the others; adopt the newest completed staged file of a wanted transfer; resume the newest partial that has a sidecar; discard everything else. Staging GC at launch and in maintenance is passed every transfer the engine still works on: jobs (those verifying or committing, i.e. with a publication in flight, included), draining attempts and launch resumes.
- **Consequences.** `R-CLN-3: staging GC during maintenance keeps the file of a publication in flight, however old` (the claimed file is aged past `stagingClaimGrace`; with the claims removed the test fails with a second download, checked by hand in this session).

## F-8 — Legacy migration loop (R9, RE-S29)
- **Decision.** After `open()` reports `legacyMigrationPending`, one owned task calls `migrateLegacy(maxFiles: 64)` while the engine runs and `!finished`; a call that leaves `remaining == 0 && !finished` (phase 4 could not finish, or phase 3 was interrupted) defers to the next maintenance window, which restarts the loop; a throwing call defers likewise. The launch run waits for the loop (it would otherwise download what migration is about to adopt). A resolve or lease of a legacy id not yet adopted calls `adoptLegacyNow` first.
- **Consequences.** `R-STO-11: the launch run waits for legacy migration and downloads nothing it adopted`; `R-STO-11: a resolve during migration adopts that legacy file first and never waits for the whole catalog` (the migration is held at its first file throughout).

## F-9 — Apple-tier gating on the engine's own conditions (E-17) and the engine half of R-NET-8
- **Decision.** Apple-tier transfers and update-checking manifest fetches wait for the engine's first `DeviceConditions` and start only when the lane's `NetworkAccess` admits them (the HTTP tier attempts and reports offline itself). A refused or invalid resume (`resumeRejected`) discards the partial and its sidecar, and the next attempt starts from zero; after a resumed attempt ends, the earlier attempt's sidecar is discarded too (found by `R-STO-1: a cancelled, paused or failed foreground transfer leaves no byte outside the store root`, failing test first).
- **Consequences.** Tests: `R-SYN-7: Apple-tier transfers and update-checking manifest fetches start only when the engine's DeviceConditions satisfy their NetworkAccess`, `R-NET-8: a refused or invalid resume discards the partial and restarts from zero in the next attempt`, `R-NET-8: an invalid resume yields resumeRejected and the engine's next attempt starts fresh`.

## F-10 — An engine without a remote source fails fetches at once
- **Context.** When `makeRemoteSource` throws, the engine still serves what is present, but a waiter for an absent asset hung (failing test first, commit `12124e8`).
- **Decision.** The construction error is kept; runs and fetches of absent assets fail with it at once (`missingSourceError`); resolves of present assets still succeed.

## F-11 — Log lines carry no identifiers or error details (R-OBS-1)
- **Decision.** Engine log lines name the failure class and a hashed identifier (`AssetLog.hashed`), never an asset id, a URL or an error's payload; details go to the diagnostics ring (32 entries, oldest dropped, `R-OBS-3: the diagnostics keep the last 32 errors`). The package-wide R-OBS-1 scan found the earlier interpolations.

## F-12 — Benchmarks (P-4, P-7, P-11, P-14)
- **Decision.** Thresholds from `QualityBudgets` only. P-4: median of 21 plans after 3 warm-ups at 10,000 and 20,000 entries with 10 % churn. P-7: a synthetic catalog streamed by `stageGenerated` into real files under `.build-tests/` with the resident size sampled at every transfer start; a quarter catalog first, so "does not grow with catalog size" compares two deltas. P-11: the real HTTP source against the loopback server with real latency, concurrency 1 against the configured concurrency (benchmarks may sleep, sync-2 S2-14); the timed sync starts after `start()`, so it includes the part of the launch run that overlaps it. P-14: a sync driven to quiescence on `ManualTimeSource`, then the census.
- **Consequences.** Release measurements in this level: P-4 11.32 ms (budget 20.0), ratio 2.02 (max 2.5); P-7 4.6 MiB for 256 MiB and 0.5 MiB for 1,024 MiB (max 48.0); P-11 speedup 3.73 (min 3.0); P-14 0 timers, 0 tasks.

## F-13 — Test harness: drive the clock without timing out a fetch
- **Decision.** `EngineHarness.drive` advances the manual clock only to the next sleeper and otherwise waits for an engine change or a new sleeper (never wall time); `syncDriven` bounds each step to half the manifest fetch deadline, because `sync` joins the launch run, whose fetch may still be in flight when the driver starts (under parallel load a jump to the deadline turned the run into a "timed out, applied manifest" run). Tests that wait for conditions events wait for the engine's state (the armed debounce), not for a replayed event.

## F-14 — Not verified
- **OI-2.** `FakeFileSystem`'s barrier equals a full sync, so the engine's durability-ordering claims (a publication is acknowledged only after the store's commit; reconcile is committed before the first transfer) are verified against that model only, not against a device's `F_BARRIERFSYNC` behaviour.
- Device-only behaviour: real background `URLSession` relaunches, real On-Demand Resources packs (R-APL-3 runs end to end through the engine, the store and `AppleHostedRemoteSource` over WP-F's `FakePackBackend` since the close-out, F-21; a real Apple-hosted pack is not exercised), real `NWPathMonitor`, thermal and Low Power Mode signals; the engine's handling of each is tested through fakes.
- P-11 includes part of the launch run (F-12); its speedup is a lower bound of the steady-state one.
- **Binding-ledger windows without a deterministic probe (L4-R5; F-21, F-22, F-23).** The resolve registration's and the prefetch join's ledger checks (a commit the engine processes between a store read and the registration; mutants M-RESOLVEREG and M-JOIN pass the suite): the window lies between two consecutive store calls with no executor unit or sink step between them, and the on-demand legacy adoption answers from the store's own location read at its end, so holding its unit never reaches the registration. Since close-out cycle 3 these checks are an optimisation: a job they let through asks the store before its first attempt (`answeredFromStore`, f-11) and downloads nothing for an id the store serves, so the two mutants no longer change what is downloaded (they cost one job and one read per id). What stays unverified is the residual window shared by the 404, the integrity and the pre-transfer reads: one await between the last presence read and the store write (`recordFailure`, `recordIntegrityFailure`) or the transfer, in which only a legacy adoption or an apply's adoption of an orphaned blob of the same content can bind the id (single flight rules out any other binding of the content while its job exists); the store guard was not added until close-out cycle 11 (F-22), and since then (L4-R16, F-31) it closes the window for `recordFailure`; `recordIntegrityFailure` and the pre-transfer read keep it. The ledger's invalidation after a store mutation that throws is shown for an `apply` whose index commit fails (PROBE-C1-N1); for an `apply` cancelled while it waits for the commit window (PROBE-C1-N2 did not reach the state), a purge, a maintenance step and a quarantine that throw, it holds by construction (the same call on each throwing path), and since cycle 3 a dropped ledger can no longer cost a download (F-23). The guard against a store view older than the copy (`followStoreView`) and the forget serial (`ledgerForgets`) are pinned at the function level (`R-SYN-12: a store view read before an apply the engine followed never takes the engine's copy back`); the interleavings that would need them (an apply's continuation running before a catch-up's, a forget landing inside a read) have no deterministic seam.
- **The ledger's job avoidance (close-out cycle 4, F-24).** Pinned since cycle 4 by the count of jobs the engine created (`jobsCreated`) in `R-SYN-6: a run does not download an asset another interest committed after its plan, and counts it as fetched` and `R-SYN-10: ensureAvailable does not download a member another interest committed after its plan`: mutant M-NOLEDGER (`isBound` always `false`, so every interest creates a job) fails both, where before cycle 4 the whole suite passed under it. The resolve-registration and prefetch-join checks (M-RESOLVEREG, M-JOIN) stay unpinned for the reason above; without them an interest costs one job and one store read per id, never a download.
- **Two committed applies whose engine turns run out of order (close-out cycle 4, by reading; produced and fixed in cycle 5, F-25).** The cycle-4 correctness verifier produced it (PROBE-C4-4, PROBE-C4-5: a run's `apply` and an ensure's, the ensure's continuation run first by a busy engine actor): the copy went back to the older manifest, a prune was announced twice and the newer manifest's additions were announced pruned. Since cycle 5 the engine sends one `apply` at a time to the store and the next only after it followed the previous one (`takeApplyTurn`, f-13), so its continuations can no longer run out of commit order; `R-SYN-12: two committed applies are followed in the store's order, so a prune is announced once and a later apply's additions never` orders the two with a test seam (it failed 6 of 6 runs before the fix; mutant M-FIFO fails it). What stays unverified is the real-priority form (a higher-priority continuation overtaking on a busy actor): the seam stands in for it, and the fix does not depend on priorities.
- **Bytes pending reclaim that the store schedules nothing for (close-out cycle 5, F-25).** The engine side is verified deterministically with a snapshot sink that does not acknowledge installs; the interleaving the cycle-4 verifier traced (the alarm's `runDueWork()` before the next fetch, after an `apply` that threw past its in-memory eviction) was not reproduced on purpose: waiting for the store to settle after such an `apply` made it reclaim the bytes in every run tried here, so which blocker held them in the verifier's run is WP-B's to narrow down (reported in the paragraph above F-1).
- **A purge's reaper running before the engine's alarm (close-out cycle 5, PROBE-C5-4b; fixed in cycle 6, F-26).** Verified deterministically with a store whose clock passes the purge's grace before the engine's clock reaches the alarm (`EngineHarness.make(storeTime:)`), which stands in for the verifier's busy engine actor; the busy-actor interleaving itself (and its order against the purge's own continuation) is not driven by a test. Either order now ends in a fresh free-space read before any `insufficientSpace` (F-26).
- **Two alarm runs whose store work overlaps (close-out cycle 5, by reading; not produced).** A deadline armed during one alarm run's `runDueWork()` starts a second alarm; the store work in flight is now counted (`storeWorkRuns`), so the first run to end no longer marks the other's work over. The cycle-5 verifier's probe (PROBE-C5-5) was inconclusive and no seam starts a second alarm inside the first's await deterministically, so no test pins the count; a settlement that would have followed the early end is decided on a fresh read since cycle 6 in any case.
- **Bytes reclaimed by maintenance (close-out cycle 6, by reading; not a finding).** Orphan collection and quota eviction in `runMaintenance` may free bytes outside the alarm too; jobs parked for space are re-checked then at the store's next deadline or the next free-space read, not at once as after a purge (F-26). Never a false shortage: a job with no wake left is decided on a fresh read.
- **A settlement read that races a newly armed store deadline (close-out cycle 6's correctness verifier; by reading, not produced).** A job parked `awaitingSpace` whose settlement read (`settleSpaceWaitsOnFreshRead`) still finds it short while a store deadline or store work was armed during the read stays parked for that wake (`EngineTimers.swift`, the `continue` after `spaceWakeExpected`). The harness has no seam that arms a store deadline inside the settlement's one read, so no test pins that branch (F-27); by reading, each `continue` with a wake expected leads to a later settlement, by the alarm's store work or by the deadline's re-read.

- **A manifest copy that changes between two join batches of a prefetch group (close-out cycle 8, F-28; by construction, not produced).** `joinGroup` re-reads the store for the members left when the engine's copy changed during a yield; ordering a manifest change between two batches needs a seam inside the join, which the harness lacks. The branch is uncovered (count 0 in this cycle's coverage run).
- **C2 at 10,000 jobs outside the space ledger (close-out cycle 8, F-28; bounded in cycle 9, F-29).** Going offline, coming back, a closed breaker or a cooldown's end, an ended throttle, a prefetch handle's release and a free-space read that re-admits are bounded per actor turn since cycle 9 and measured by the release benchmarks against C2's 10 ms at 10,000 members. Still linear in one turn, and not measured at 10,000: the first turn of each of those events collects and sorts its candidates (O(n log n) over the index, a few milliseconds at 10,000; about ten times that at 100,000, which SPEC §6 does not set as a reference scale for the engine), `registerGroup`'s de-duplication and sort of the identifiers (about 3 ms at 10,000), the retries that come due together in one alarm turn (`fireDue`; reaching thousands needs thousands of attempts failed with a backoff and a clock jump past them all), and shutdown. Since close-out cycle 10 (F-30) three more are measured by release benchmarks: a cooldown's end over 10,000 members the breaker parked (4.74 to 5.13 ms, the re-queues' first turn collecting and sorting every owed member), the release of a user-initiated handle (4.06 to 4.34 ms; 22.0 to 22.2 ms before cycle 10's set-aside), and a `resolve` caller's cancellation beside 10,000 queued members, which `withdraw` no longer answers with a sorted pass over every job (0.02 ms; 5.28 to 6.25 ms before, and the cycle-9 requirements verifier's PROBE-C9R-1 measured 5.79, 6.28 and 6.44 ms at a 1-minute load about 7.5).
- **The lane-park continuation (close-out cycle 9, F-29; pinned in cycle 10, F-30).** `laneParksOwed` lets later pumps finish a lane-wide park whether or not a slot frees. Mutant M-LANEOWED (no continuation) passed the suite until cycle 10, because when the network drops nothing else can start and the slot a failed attempt freed lets the pump's own follow-up continue the park; the continuation decides only when other lanes take every freed slot between the turns. Since cycle 10 `R-SYN-7: a lane-wide policy park that stops at its turn's bound reaches every queued member even when user work takes the freed slot` constructs that interleaving in one actor turn (Low Data Mode, a released user handle's slot, then user work taking it) and fails under M-LANEOWED (64 members stay queued).
- **A 404's waiters for an id bound during `recordFailure`'s await (close-out cycle 12, F-32; by reading, not produced).** Since L4-R16 the store leaves such an id present and the engine forgets its binding, so the next resolve reads the store; but `failed` (`permanentMissing`) still answers the job's waiters for that id `notFound` (`resolveAll(transfer, with: .failed(.notFound))`, `EngineFailures.swift`) while the store serves it. The window is the one await of `recordFailure` after f-10's presence reads; no executor unit or sink step sits in it, so no probe produced it (F-22). A caller that asks again is answered from the store. For ADR-053 and VERIFICATION "Not verified".

## F-15 — A run whose manifest fetch timed out writes no stamp (fix round 1; needs a ruling)
- **Context.** Verifier finding: a run whose fetch passed `manifestFetchDeadline` ran on the applied manifest, ended `.complete` and wrote the stamp, so under `.oncePerAppVersion` a slow-network launch of a new version marked the version synced without fetching its manifest.
- **Options.** (a) Keep stamping; (b) end `.completeWithFailures` (with no failure listed); (c) end `.complete` with `manifestFresh == false` and write no stamp (and leave `syncedThisLaunch` false).
- **Decision.** (c): the outcome still says what happened (the applied manifest is complete locally, `report.manifestFresh` says it was not refreshed), and the sync stays due under every policy, so the next due check fetches again. ARCHITECTURE §7.10's "markSynced only for `.complete`" now reads "only for a `.complete` run with a fresh manifest".
- **Consequences.** `R-SYN-6: a manifest fetch past its deadline falls back to the applied manifest and leaves the sync due` (a second lifetime of a new app version; `isSyncDue()` stays true).

## F-16 — Offline runs retry once on the debounce, then wait for a network edge (R-SYN-13; fix round 1)
- **Context.** Verifier PROBE-7: a run that ends offline while the engine's conditions read the path satisfied (URLSession says -1009/-1018/-1020 under some VPN or roaming paths) set a conditions-gated pending run that bulk admission scheduled after `pendingRunDebounce`; the next run failed the same way, for ever: one manifest request per debounce with no conditions change (10 requests over 10 timer cycles).
- **Options.** (a) Open the conditions gate only on a satisfied-path edge after any offline failure; (b) back off pending-run retries exponentially (still a poll, every 300 s at most); (c) one debounced retry, then edge-only.
- **Decision.** (c). `EngineState.offlineRunsWithoutEdge` counts runs in a row that ended offline (reset by a satisfied-path edge and by any run that ends otherwise); the first is retried after the debounce once conditions admit bulk work (a path monitor often reports satisfied a moment before connections work, or its first element was not in yet); a later one that ends while the path reads satisfied sets `PendingRun.awaitsNetworkEdge`, which `considerPendingRun` and the gate state honour until `apply(conditions)` sees an edge. (a) alone broke `R-SYN-6: a pending run whose gate opens while the blocked run is still finishing starts once it has finished`, whose single offline failure must still be retried.
- **Consequences.** At most one automatic retry without an edge, then no timer at all (`R-SYN-13: runs that keep failing offline while the path reads satisfied run again only after a network edge, never on a timer`, failing first: the old code scheduled the debounce again). The pending run keeps the conditions watcher (work), so an edge is seen.

## F-17 — Two test budgets (fix round 1)
- **Decision.** `R-SYN-12: a stalled subscriber's backlog never exceeds one slot per progress key and subject during a 10,000-asset sync` gets `.timeLimit(.minutes(3))`: 9.5 s on macOS, but 58.4 s under the thread sanitizer and 35.8 to 57.3 s on the iOS Simulator under load (an overrun restarts the runner, `ios.md` item 8); no assertion changed. `R-STO-1: a cancelled, paused or failed foreground transfer leaves no byte outside the store root` compares the `CFNetworkDownload_*` files created since the test began whose leading bytes are a run of one of the test's bodies, instead of the process-wide count, which other suites running in parallel on the simulator disturb; an empty file holds no byte, so the requirement checked is unchanged.
- **Close-out cycle 1 (L4-R8; L4-V3-03, L4-V3-15).** The verifier measured the 10,000-asset test in the full sanitizer suite at 115.3 s (load average about 15 to 25) and 151.0 s (load rising to 73), against 180 s. This cycle cut its cost (F-21: one slot pass per `pump` round, `checkRunSettled` stops at the first working claim, the backlog read from the queue's high-water mark instead of a sampler after every mutation) and raised the limit to `.minutes(4)` (240 s). Measured on this host after the change (the sanitizer build of `ZTronAssetSyncTests`): alone 15.1 s at load average 2 to 3 (17.8 s before the change, same host and load); in the whole package's sanitizer suite 17.5 s at load 3.8 to 11 (each test bundle runs in its own process, one after another, so "in-suite" means alongside the other 213 Sync tests); in the Sync sanitizer suite run beside this WP's own iOS Simulator test run, 72.5 s at load average 79 to 141 (median 119), 30 % of the new limit; 0 ThreadSanitizer reports in every run. A load around 25 was not produced on purpose; at about five times that load the test used 30 % of its limit, which bounds its time at a load around 25 from above (an inference from the measurements, not a measurement at 25). On the iOS 27 Simulator it took 8.6 s (load average 12 to 65); debug alone 3.8 to 3.9 s. `R-SYN-6: a pending run whose gate opens while the blocked run is still finishing starts once it has finished` now takes 1.4 to 1.5 s under the sanitizer in-suite (two cases; 55.9 s and a time-limit overrun before) and keeps its 1-minute limit.
- **Close-out cycle 2 (the requirements verifier's loaded figures, L4-R8).** The 10,000-asset test, in-suite: under the sanitizer 17.9 s at load average 6 to 11.5 and 20.8 s at 21.9 to 48.1 (median 31.9), 8.7 % of its 240 s limit, so L4-R8's at most 50 % at a load around 25 is met; in debug 5.6 s at load about 7, 29.8 s at 9 to 12 and 101.4 s at a 1-minute load of 307 (42 %); on the iOS Simulator 13.5 s at a median load of 41 and 60.3 s at 174. It slows with load far more than the other Sync tests (each under 10 s in those runs); no overrun was observed, and one under the sanitizer at a load of 150 or more is plausible. Nothing changed: the asset count is the requirement's scale, and no assertion or limit moved. This cycle on this host: 16.9 s under the sanitizer in-suite at load 4.6 falling to 2.9.
- **Close-out cycle 3 (L4-R8; the cycle-2 verifiers' figures and this cycle's).** The requirements verifier of cycle 2 measured the test in-suite under the sanitizer at 143.15 s with a 1-minute load of 17.35, 21.01, 25.51 and 14.00 during the run (8 CPUs; three debug Sync runs and another verifier's chain ran at the same time), 59.6 % of the 240 s limit, and 16.86 s at a load of 4.2 to 5.0; the correctness verifier measured 19.0 s (load 8.4 to 14.2) and 16.2 s (load about 1.5) under the sanitizer, 18.1 s, 59.7 s and 8.2 s in debug (loads 7 to 21) and 14.6 s on the iOS 27 Simulator. No overrun. F-17's cycle-2 figure (20.8 s at a load of 21.9 to 48.1) therefore does not bound the time under concurrent `swift test` contention: under that contention at a load around 20 the 50 % margin of L4-R8 was not met once. The cause is outside the test's own path as far as profiles show (F-21: about half of the sanitizer time is the store's publication path, where WP-B's `gainReferenceInPlace` copies the blob table per publication in debug and sanitizer builds, `outside-wp`), and the time is load-only (the rulings' process rule: a budget miss caused only by load is not blocking). Measured this cycle after its code changes (`4038f7f`; the pre-transfer presence read adds one store hop per attempt): debug in-suite 4.02, 4.02 and 3.96 s (load 3.4 to 4.9); sanitizer in-suite 17.4 s at load 3.2 to 4.1 (0 reports), and 48.6 s at a 1-minute load of 44.5 to 68.9 (median 67.0, 17 samples) while this WP's iOS Simulator run was under way, 20.3 % of the limit; at the cycle's last code commit (`73d20b6`) 16.97 s at load 2.8 to 4.3 and 67.4 s at a 1-minute load of 41.8 to 95.1 (median 81.5) beside the simulator run, 28.1 %; debug 3.96 to 3.97 s; iOS Simulator 10.5 s. Not done: scaling the asset count down under the sanitizer, which the finding offered as optional; the display name states the 10,000-asset scale and a smaller count would make it untrue in that build.
- **Close-out cycle 4 (L4-R8; the cycle-3 verifiers' figures and this cycle's).** No overrun and no flake. The requirements verifier of cycle 3 measured the test in-suite beside its own iOS Simulator run: debug 97.39, 51.92 and 20.79 s (1-minute load 5.5 to 161.8, median 38.2; the largest is 40.6 % of 240 s), under the sanitizer 34.22 s (load 14.1 to 54.3, median 31.8; 14.3 %), on the iOS 27 Simulator 18.83 s. The correctness verifier: debug 54.0 s (load rising from 14.5 to 135; 22.5 %), 21.6 s (load about 112 to 135) and 7.6 s (about 101 to 112), under the sanitizer 17.2 s (load 2.1 to 7.0; 7.2 %), on the simulator 11.7 s. This cycle's figures are in F-24. No limit, count or assertion changed.
- **Close-out cycle 5 (L4-R8; the cycle-4 verifiers' figures and this cycle's).** No overrun and no flake of this test. The requirements verifier of cycle 4 measured it in-suite: under the sanitizer 145.96 s beside its own iOS Simulator run at a 1-minute load of 12.4 to 96.6 (median 80.6), 60.8 % of the 240 s limit (load-only: the process rule makes it non-blocking), and 35.53 s at 18.2 to 36.8 (median 26.4), 14.8 %, which meets L4-R8's at most 50 % at a load around 25; debug 80.07 s in `--quick` at about 7.7 to 19 and 4.49, 4.08 and 4.09 s in the three Sync runs (5.1 to 14.6); iOS 27 Simulator 10.99 s. The correctness verifier: debug 30.89 s (20.6 to 30.6, probes beside it), 7.12 and 4.71 s (about 17 to 18); sanitizer 19.94 s at 7.7 to 16.2 (8.3 %), 0 reports; iOS Simulator 19.55 s at 11.4 to 161.8 (median 85.5). This cycle's figures are in F-25. No limit, count or assertion changed.
- **Close-out cycle 6 (L4-R8; the cycle-5 verifiers' figures and this cycle's).** No flake: no run recorded "Time limit was exceeded". The requirements verifier of cycle 5 ran its first in-suite sanitizer run beside its own `--quick`, its iOS Simulator run and the other verifier's work, at a 1-minute load of 7.3 to 195.5 (median 125.6, 27 samples): the 10,000-asset test passed after 249.198 s, 103.8 % of the 240 s limit, with no overrun recorded by Swift Testing; its second sanitizer run, at 4.5 to 6.5, took 18.311 s (7.6 %). With cycle 4's 14.8 % at a load about 26 and 60.8 % at about 80, the time grows with load alone, and L4-R8's at most 50 % at a load around 25 holds; at a load past 100 the margin is gone, so a future overrun there would count as a flake under L4-R8 (**for the orchestrator:** concurrent verifier runs, each with a simulator, push the host's load past 100). Its other figures: debug 39.83, 7.31 and 7.95 s in the three Sync runs (load 19.8 to 33.1), the `--quick` Sync suite 76.84 s (median 97), the iOS 27 Simulator 104.51 s (median 125.6), 32 stress runs 4.01 to 5.15 s (2 to 5). The correctness verifier: debug 8.29, 47.79 and 11.95 s (17.8 to 23.9), under the sanitizer in-suite 17.49 s (7.3 %, 3.4 to 5.0, 0 reports), on the iOS 27 Simulator 7.83 s (1.9 to 141, median 63). This cycle's figures are in F-26. No limit, count or assertion changed.
- **Close-out cycle 7 (L4-R8; the cycle-6 verifiers' figures and this cycle's).** No flake: no run recorded "Time limit was exceeded". The requirements verifier of cycle 6 measured the 10,000-asset test in-suite under the sanitizer at 125.038 s (52.1 % of the 240 s limit) at a 1-minute load of 15.71 to 28.07 (median 22.73, 13 samples) while its run overlapped its own iOS build, a stress compile and the other verifier's work as the host climbed toward a load of 400, and at 18.197 s (7.6 %) at a load of 2.10 to 2.63; `--quick` 32.343 s; three Sync runs 4.174, 4.123 and 4.156 s (load 7.0 to 7.8); the coverage run 162.326 s (load median about 270); iOS Simulator 183.942 s (median load 270) and 11.657 s (median 62.8). The correctness verifier of cycle 6: debug 4.159, 4.504 and 6.364 s (load 5.1 to 11.6); `--quick` 32.343 s; under the sanitizer 36.792 s, 15.3 % (load 7.7 to 27.6, median 21.0), no sanitizer report; the coverage run 143.608 s under heavy concurrent load; the iOS 27 Simulator 152.452 s, 63.5 % (load 19.6 to 468.9, median 293). The 52.1 % figure is the only one above L4-R8's 50 % near a load of 25, and its 1-minute samples lag a host that was climbing far past it; the quiet sanitizer runs stay under 8 %. No limit, count or assertion changed (L4-R8 allows a limit change only with a cost cut, and nothing overran). For the orchestrator: concurrent verifier runs, each with a simulator, push the load past 100 to about 470, where the margin shrinks to about 1.6x; serialising the verifiers' simulator runs keeps it. This cycle's figures: debug in-suite 4.136 s (`--quick`, 1-minute load 2.18 to 7.70, median 5.04), 4.167, 4.124 and 4.101 s (three Sync runs, load 1.70 to 2.76), 4.148 s (coverage run, load 3.37 to 6.67); under the sanitizer 18.136 s, 7.6 % of the limit (load 2.51 to 3.20, median 2.79), no report; iOS 27 Simulator 10.517 s, 4.4 % (load 2.80 to 84.41, median 54.65).
